========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve been having trouble keeping my focus at work. I’m a receptionist, so I’m scheduling appointments and answering questions, and sometimes I’ll be right in the middle of putting something in the calendar or working on the lobby displays, and I just… lose my place. Like my mind slips off to thinking about what I might have missed or overlooked, and then I have to backtrack and check what I was doing.

It’s not like I can’t do my job, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me. I’ll be trying to concentrate, and then I’m mentally going through, “Did I forget to call someone back? Did I put that in the right spot? Did I miss a detail?” and then I realize I’ve stopped paying attention to the thing right in front of me. That’s really why I’m here.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. More than I think I should, honestly. It’s not always one huge thing, either — it’s all these ordinary things that somehow feel like they could go wrong if I don’t keep turning them over in my head.

Like at work, I’ll worry about the schedule, whether I put someone in the right slot or if I missed a note. Or with bills, even if they’re handled, I’ll still think, “What if I forgot one?” If I’m making travel plans, even something simple, I start going through every possible mix-up. And little paperwork errors — things that probably would be fixable — they feel like they could turn into some much bigger problem. I know, logically, that one small mistake usually isn’t the end of the world, but in the moment my mind treats it like it could snowball.

And little paperwork errors — things that probably would be fixable — they feel like they could turn into some much bigger problem. I know, logically, that one small mistake usually isn’t the end of the world, but in the moment my mind treats it like it could snowball.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, like a sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I mean, I can feel very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it builds up and I’ll realize I’m really tense or on edge, but it’s more like it keeps running in the background and ramps up depending on what I’m thinking about.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things exactly… not in a really obvious way, anyway. I still go to work, I still answer the phones, deal with people at the front desk, handle paperwork — I don’t just refuse to do things because I’m worried.

But I guess sometimes I’ll put things off a little if I’m worried I’m going to mess them up, like I’ll want to check details first or wait until I feel like I have enough time to do it carefully. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.

So… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.

Like I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.

I don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.

I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like a very specific act I have to do a certain number of times or anything like counting or touching things or following a rule.

It’s more ordinary checking, I guess — like if I’m unsure whether I wrote something down correctly, I might look again, or if I’m worried I misunderstood something, I might review it. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.

So… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.

looping, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another until I’m checking details in my head over and over.\n- key: S058\n  name: Restlessness_Tension\n  description: Restlessness, or feeling chief_complaint_symptom_code: S059\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things exactly… not in a really obvious way, anyway. I still go to work, I still answer the phones, deal with people at the front desk, handle paperwork — I don’t just refuse to do things because I’m worried.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I’ll put things off a little if I’m worried I’m going to mess them up, like I’ll want to check details first or wait until I feel like I have enough time to do it carefully. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.\n\nI don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific acts, like checking or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like a very specific act I have to do a certain number of times or anything like counting or touching things or following a rule.\n\nIt’s more ordinary checking, I guess — like if I’m unsure whether I wrote something down correctly, I might look again, or if I’m worried I misunderstood something, I might review it. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre your worries usually about one specific type of thing or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They’re definitely spread out. It’s not just one thing, like only money or only work. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”

At work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. Then at home it might be bills, or making sure a travel plan is right, or whether I forgot some small detail that’s going to snowball into something bigger.

It’s usually not dramatic things, either. It’s everyday things. But in my head it’s like one small mix-up could turn into a whole chain of problems, and then I start trying to think through every possible way it could go wrong.

about all kinds of ordinary things now—work schedules, bills, travel plans, even little paperwork errors—as if one small mix-up could turn into a much bigger problem.\n- key: S057\n  name: Uncontrollable_Worry\n  description: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.\n  manifestation: I can tell myself, ‘Sunny, it’s handled,’ but the thought keeps looping, and pretty soon I’ve gone from one concern to another until I’m checking details in my head over and over.\n- key: S058\n  name: Restlessness_Tension\n  description: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.\n  manifestation: At the front desk I can still smile and help people, but inside I feel wound tight, like my shoulders are up around my ears and I’m waiting for something to go wrong.\n- key: S059\n  name: Concentration_Difficulty\n  description: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.\n  manifestation: What made me come in is that I’ve started losing my place while I’m scheduling appointments or working on the lobby displays, like my mind slips off track because I’m busy thinking about what I might have overlooked.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up and getting through the day, but scheduling takes me longer now because I double-check myself so much, and I’ve had to redo parts of the lobby displays after losing track of what I was arranging.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about nine months, ever since that overnight detention and the paperwork mix-up around it.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 44 / receptionist_or_information_clerk / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S059\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.\n\nI don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific acts, like checking or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like a very specific act I have to do a certain number of times or anything like counting or touching things or following a rule.\n\nIt’s more ordinary checking, I guess — like if I’m unsure whether I wrote something down correctly, I might look again, or if I’m worried I misunderstood something, I might review it. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about one specific type of thing or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely spread out. It’s not just one thing, like only money or only work. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”\n\nAt work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. Then at home it might be bills, or making sure a travel plan is right, or whether I forgot some small detail that’s going to snowball into something bigger.\n\nIt’s usually not dramatic things, either. It’s everyday things. But in my head it’s like one small mix-up could turn into a whole chain of problems, and then I start trying to think through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall quality of life or relationships with others?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or not able to do my job, because I do show up, I get through the day, I answer people, I do what I’m supposed to do.

But it does interfere, just in these smaller ways that add up. Like scheduling takes me longer than it should now because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at an appointment or a calendar entry and think, “Okay, that’s right,” and then a minute later I’m back checking the same thing again because I don’t trust that I didn’t miss something. So what should be quick ends up taking extra time, and then I feel behind or embarrassed, even if nobody else notices right away.

And there have been times with the lobby displays where I’m arranging things, and because I’m so in my head or going back over details, I lose track of what I was doing. Then I have to redo parts of it because I realize it doesn’t look right or I forgot what order I was putting things in. It’s not huge, but it’s frustrating. It makes the day feel harder than it needs to be.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve been having trouble keeping my focus at work. I’m a receptionist, so I’m scheduling appointments and answering questions, and sometimes I’ll be right in the middle of putting something in the calendar or working on the lobby displays, and I just… lose my place. Like my mind slips off to thinking about what I might have missed or overlooked, and then I have to backtrack and check what I was doing.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me. I’ll be trying to concentrate, and then I’m mentally going through, “Did I forget to call someone back? Did I put that in the right spot? Did I miss a detail?” and then I realize I’ve stopped paying attention to the thing right in front of me. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I think I should, honestly. It’s not always one huge thing, either — it’s all these ordinary things that somehow feel like they could go wrong if I don’t keep turning them over in my head.\n\nLike at work, I’ll worry about the schedule, whether I put someone in the right slot or if I missed a note. Or with bills, even if they’re handled, I’ll still think, “What if I forgot one?” If I’m making travel plans, even something simple, I start going through every possible mix-up. And little paperwork errors — things that probably would be fixable — they feel like they could turn into some much bigger problem. I know, logically, that one small mistake usually isn’t the end of the world, but in the moment my mind treats it like it could snowball.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — especially tension. I don’t always notice it right away because I’m still doing my job, you know, smiling at people at the front desk, answering the phone, trying to sound calm and pleasant. But inside I feel really wound tight, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong.\n\nA lot of the time my shoulders are up around my ears without me realizing it. Then at some point I’ll catch myself and try to drop them, but they just creep right back up. It’s like my body is waiting for the next mistake or problem, even if nothing is actually happening in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, like a sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I mean, I can feel very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it builds up and I’ll realize I’m really tense or on edge, but it’s more like it keeps running in the background and ramps up depending on what I’m thinking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had those moments where it’s like, all at once, “Something terrible is happening right now,” and then it peaks within minutes. At least, not that I can clearly point to. Mine feels more drawn out, like I get stuck in the worrying and checking and replaying things, rather than a sudden intense fear spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things exactly… not in a really obvious way, anyway. I still go to work, I still answer the phones, deal with people at the front desk, handle paperwork — I don’t just refuse to do things because I’m worried.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I’ll put things off a little if I’m worried I’m going to mess them up, like I’ll want to check details first or wait until I feel like I have enough time to do it carefully. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.

I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.

And the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.

I can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve been having trouble keeping my focus at work. I’m a receptionist, so I’m scheduling appointments and answering questions, and sometimes I’ll be right in the middle of putting something in the calendar or working on the lobby displays, and I just… lose my place. Like my mind slips off to thinking about what I might have missed or overlooked, and then I have to backtrack and check what I was doing.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me. I’ll be trying to concentrate, and then I’m mentally going through, “Did I forget to call someone back? Did I put that in the right spot? Did I miss a detail?” and then I realize I’ve stopped paying attention to the thing right in front of me. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I think I should, honestly. It’s not always one huge thing, either — it’s all these ordinary things that somehow feel like they could go wrong if I don’t keep turning them over in my head.\n\nLike at work, I’ll worry about the schedule, whether I put someone in the right slot or if I missed a note. Or with bills, even if they’re handled, I’ll still think, “What if I forgot one?” If I’m making travel plans, even something simple, I start going through every possible mix-up. And little paperwork errors — things that probably would be fixable — they feel like they could turn into some much bigger problem. I know, logically, that one small mistake usually isn’t the end of the world, but in the moment my mind treats it like it could snowball.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — especially tension. I don’t always notice it right away because I’m still doing my job, you know, smiling at people at the front desk, answering the phone, trying to sound calm and pleasant. But inside I feel really wound tight, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong.\n\nA lot of the time my shoulders are up around my ears without me realizing it. Then at some point I’ll catch myself and try to drop them, but they just creep right back up. It’s like my body is waiting for the next mistake or problem, even if nothing is actually happening in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, like a sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I mean, I can feel very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it builds up and I’ll realize I’m really tense or on edge, but it’s more like it keeps running in the background and ramps up depending on what I’m thinking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had those moments where it’s like, all at once, “Something terrible is happening right now,” and then it peaks within minutes. At least, not that I can clearly point to. Mine feels more drawn out, like I get stuck in the worrying and checking and replaying things, rather than a sudden intense fear spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things exactly… not in a really obvious way, anyway. I still go to work, I still answer the phones, deal with people at the front desk, handle paperwork — I don’t just refuse to do things because I’m worried.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I’ll put things off a little if I’m worried I’m going to mess them up, like I’ll want to check details first or wait until I feel like I have enough time to do it carefully. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.\n\nI don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific acts, like checking or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about one specific type of thing or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely spread out. I still go to work, I still answer the phones, deal with people at the front desk, handle paperwork — I don’t just refuse to do things because I’m worried.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I’ll put things off a little if I’m worried I’m going to mess them up, like I’ll want to check details first or wait until I feel like I have enough time to do it carefully. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.\n\nI don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific acts, like checking or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like a very specific act I have to do a certain number of times or anything like counting or touching things or following a rule.\n\nIt’s more ordinary checking, I guess — like if I’m unsure whether I wrote something down correctly, I might look again, or if I’m worried I misunderstood something, I might review it. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about one specific type of thing or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely spread out. It’s not just one thing, like only money or only work. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”\n\nAt work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. Then at home it might be bills, or making sure a travel plan is right, or whether I forgot some small detail that’s going to snowball into something bigger.\n\nIt’s usually not dramatic things, either. It’s everyday things. But in my head it’s like one small mix-up could turn into a whole chain of problems, and then I start trying to think through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall quality of life or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or not able to do my job, because I do show up, I get through the day, I answer people, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it does interfere, just in these smaller ways that add up. Like scheduling takes me longer than it should now because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at an appointment or a calendar entry and think, “Okay, that’s right,” and then a minute later I’m back checking the same thing again because I don’t trust that I didn’t miss something. So what should be quick ends up taking extra time, and then I feel behind or embarrassed, even if nobody else notices right away.\n\nAnd there have been times with the lobby displays where I’m arranging things, and because I’m so in my head or going back over details, I lose track of what I was doing. Then I have to redo parts of it because I realize it doesn’t look right or I forgot what order I was putting things in. It’s not huge, but it’s frustrating. It makes the day feel harder than it needs to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.\n\nAnd the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.\n\nI can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. Like at the front desk, I can still smile and greet people and answer the phone and help them with whatever they need, so I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.

It’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. I’ll notice my shoulders are all tense, almost like they’re up around my ears, and I’m sitting there waiting for something to go wrong. Not even a specific thing necessarily, just this feeling like I need to be ready. And then if someone walks up suddenly or the phone rings, I’m already kind of on edge before I even know what they need.

So yes, I guess restless is the right word, but it’s more like tense and keyed up than pacing around or anything dramatic. I’m still doing my job and talking to people, but inside I feel like I can’t fully relax.

But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I do, though it’s not always that I completely avoid the decision. It’s more that I hesitate, or I put it off, or I make it much bigger in my head than it probably needs to be.

Like with work schedules, I’ll worry that if I enter something wrong or misunderstand a time, it’s going to create this whole chain of problems for someone else. Or with bills, even ordinary ones, I’ll look at the amount or the due date and think, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are the same way. I can get stuck checking times, addresses, confirmation numbers, because in my mind one little mix-up could turn into a whole mess.

And paperwork too, even small things. If there’s a tiny error or I’m not completely sure I wrote something correctly, I can feel myself freezing a bit before I move on, because I’m imagining it turning into a bigger problem later. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.

I’m a receptionist, so I’m scheduling appointments and answering questions, and sometimes I’ll be right in the middle of putting something in the calendar or working on the lobby displays, and I just… lose my place. Like my mind slips off to thinking about what I might have missed or overlooked, and then I have to backtrack and check what I was doing.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me. I’ll be trying to concentrate, and then I’m mentally going through, “Did I forget to call someone back? Did I put that in the right spot? Did I miss a detail?” and then I realize I’ve stopped paying attention to the thing right in front of me. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I think I should, honestly. It’s not always one huge thing, either — it’s all these ordinary things that somehow feel like they could go wrong if I don’t keep turning them over in my head.\n\nLike at work, I’ll worry about the schedule, whether I put someone in the right slot or if I missed a note. Or with bills, even if they’re handled, I’ll still think, “What if I forgot one?” If I’m making travel plans, even something simple, I start going through every possible mix-up. And little paperwork errors — things that probably would be fixable — they feel like they could turn into some much bigger problem. I know, logically, that one small mistake usually isn’t the end of the world, but in the moment my mind treats it like it could snowball.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — especially tension. I don’t always notice it right away because I’m still doing my job, you know, smiling at people at the front desk, answering the phone, trying to sound calm and pleasant. But inside I feel really wound tight, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong.\n\nA lot of the time my shoulders are up around my ears without me realizing it. Then at some point I’ll catch myself and try to drop them, but they just creep right back up. It’s like my body is waiting for the next mistake or problem, even if nothing is actually happening in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, like a sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I mean, I can feel very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it builds up and I’ll realize I’m really tense or on edge, but it’s more like it keeps running in the background and ramps up depending on what I’m thinking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had those moments where it’s like, all at once, “Something terrible is happening right now,” and then it peaks within minutes. At least, not that I can clearly point to. Mine feels more drawn out, like I get stuck in the worrying and checking and replaying things, rather than a sudden intense fear spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things exactly… not in a really obvious way, anyway. I still go to work, I still answer the phones, deal with people at the front desk, handle paperwork — I don’t just refuse to do things because I’m worried.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I’ll put things off a little if I’m worried I’m going to mess them up, like I’ll want to check details first or wait until I feel like I have enough time to do it carefully. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.\n\nI don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific acts, like checking or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like a very specific act I have to do a certain number of times or anything like counting or touching things or following a rule.\n\nIt’s more ordinary checking, I guess — like if I’m unsure whether I wrote something down correctly, I might look again, or if I’m worried I misunderstood something, I might review it. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about one specific type of thing or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely spread out. It’s not just one thing, like only money or only work. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”\n\nAt work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. Then at home it might be bills, or making sure a travel plan is right, or whether I forgot some small detail that’s going to snowball into something bigger.\n\nIt’s usually not dramatic things, either. It’s everyday things. But in my head it’s like one small mix-up could turn into a whole chain of problems, and then I start trying to think through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall quality of life or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or not able to do my job, because I do show up, I get through the day, I answer people, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it does interfere, just in these smaller ways that add up. Like scheduling takes me longer than it should now because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at an appointment or a calendar entry and think, “Okay, that’s right,” and then a minute later I’m back checking the same thing again because I don’t trust that I didn’t miss something. So what should be quick ends up taking extra time, and then I feel behind or embarrassed, even if nobody else notices right away.\n\nAnd there have been times with the lobby displays where I’m arranging things, and because I’m so in my head or going back over details, I lose track of what I was doing. Then I have to redo parts of it because I realize it doesn’t look right or I forgot what order I was putting things in. It’s not huge, but it’s frustrating. It makes the day feel harder than it needs to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.\n\nAnd the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.\n\nI can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. Like at the front desk, I can still smile and greet people and answer the phone and help them with whatever they need, so I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. I’ll notice my shoulders are all tense, almost like they’re up around my ears, and I’m sitting there waiting for something to go wrong. Not even a specific thing necessarily, just this feeling like I need to be ready. And then if someone walks up suddenly or the phone rings, I’m already kind of on edge before I even know what they need.\n\nSo yes, I guess restless is the right word, but it’s more like tense and keyed up than pacing around or anything dramatic. I’m still doing my job and talking to people, but inside I feel like I can’t fully relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid making decisions or taking actions because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it’s not always that I completely avoid the decision. It’s more that I hesitate, or I put it off, or I make it much bigger in my head than it probably needs to be.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll worry that if I enter something wrong or misunderstand a time, it’s going to create this whole chain of problems for someone else. Or with bills, even ordinary ones, I’ll look at the amount or the due date and think, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are the same way. I can get stuck checking times, addresses, confirmation numbers, because in my mind one little mix-up could turn into a whole mess.\n\nAnd paperwork too, even small things. If there’s a tiny error or I’m not completely sure I wrote something correctly, I can feel myself freezing a bit before I move on, because I’m imagining it turning into a bigger problem later. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

If there’s a tiny error or I’m not completely sure I wrote something correctly, I can feel myself freezing a bit before I move on, because I’m imagining it turning into a bigger problem later. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.\n\nAnd the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.\n\nI can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. Like at the front desk, I can still smile and greet people and answer the phone and help them with whatever they need, so I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. I’ll notice my shoulders are all tense, almost like they’re up around my ears, and I’m sitting there waiting for something to go wrong. Not even a specific thing necessarily, just this feeling like I need to be ready. I’m still doing my job and talking to people, but inside I feel like I can’t fully relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid making decisions or taking actions because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it’s not always that I completely avoid the decision. It’s more that I hesitate, or I put it off, or I make it much bigger in my head than it probably needs to be.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll worry that if I enter something wrong or misunderstand a time, it’s going to create this whole chain of problems for someone else. Or with bills, even ordinary ones, I’ll look at the amount or the due date and think, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are the same way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can clearly point to. My family and friends know I can be a little… careful, I guess, or that I like to double-check things, but I don’t know if they see it as a serious problem.

Sometimes I wonder if I ask for reassurance more than I realize, like, “Did I say that right?” or “Are you sure that’s the time?” But I’m not sure whether that actually bothers them or if I’m just assuming it might. People close to me are usually pretty patient, so it’s hard for me to tell.

So I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a major impact on my relationships. Maybe small things here and there, but nothing obvious enough that someone has said, “This is affecting us,” if that makes sense.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.8, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve been having trouble keeping my focus at work. I’m a receptionist, so I’m scheduling appointments and answering questions, and sometimes I’ll be right in the middle of putting something in the calendar or working on the lobby displays, and I just… lose my place. Like my mind slips off to thinking about what I might have missed or overlooked, and then I have to backtrack and check what I was doing.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me. I’ll be trying to concentrate, and then I’m mentally going through, “Did I forget to call someone back? Did I put that in the right spot? Did I miss a detail?” and then I realize I’ve stopped paying attention to the thing right in front of me. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I think I should, honestly. It’s not always one huge thing, either — it’s all these ordinary things that somehow feel like they could go wrong if I don’t keep turning them over in my head.\n\nLike at work, I’ll worry about the schedule, whether I put someone in the right slot or if I missed a note. Or with bills, even if they’re handled, I’ll still think, “What if I forgot one?” If I’m making travel plans, even something simple, I start going through every possible mix-up. And little paperwork errors — things that probably would be fixable — they feel like they could turn into some much bigger problem. I know, logically, that one small mistake usually isn’t the end of the world, but in the moment my mind treats it like it could snowball.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — especially tension. I don’t always notice it right away because I’m still doing my job, you know, smiling at people at the front desk, answering the phone, trying to sound calm and pleasant. But inside I feel really wound tight, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong.\n\nA lot of the time my shoulders are up around my ears without me realizing it. Then at some point I’ll catch myself and try to drop them, but they just creep right back up. It’s like my body is waiting for the next mistake or problem, even if nothing is actually happening in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, like a sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I mean, I can feel very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it builds up and I’ll realize I’m really tense or on edge, but it’s more like it keeps running in the background and ramps up depending on what I’m thinking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had those moments where it’s like, all at once, “Something terrible is happening right now,” and then it peaks within minutes. At least, not that I can clearly point to. Mine feels more drawn out, like I get stuck in the worrying and checking and replaying things, rather than a sudden intense fear spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things exactly… not in a really obvious way, anyway. I still go to work, I still answer the phones, deal with people at the front desk, handle paperwork — I don’t just refuse to do things because I’m worried.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I’ll put things off a little if I’m worried I’m going to mess them up, like I’ll want to check details first or wait until I feel like I have enough time to do it carefully. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.\n\nI don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific acts, like checking or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like a very specific act I have to do a certain number of times or anything like counting or touching things or following a rule.\n\nIt’s more ordinary checking, I guess — like if I’m unsure whether I wrote something down correctly, I might look again, or if I’m worried I misunderstood something, I might review it. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about one specific type of thing or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely spread out. It’s not just one thing, like only money or only work. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”\n\nAt work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. Then at home it might be bills, or making sure a travel plan is right, or whether I forgot some small detail that’s going to snowball into something bigger.\n\nIt’s usually not dramatic things, either. It’s everyday things. But in my head it’s like one small mix-up could turn into a whole chain of problems, and then I start trying to think through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall quality of life or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or not able to do my job, because I do show up, I get through the day, I answer people, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it does interfere, just in these smaller ways that add up. Like scheduling takes me longer than it should now because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at an appointment or a calendar entry and think, “Okay, that’s right,” and then a minute later I’m back checking the same thing again because I don’t trust that I didn’t miss something. So what should be quick ends up taking extra time, and then I feel behind or embarrassed, even if nobody else notices right away.\n\nAnd there have been times with the lobby displays where I’m arranging things, and because I’m so in my head or going back over details, I lose track of what I was doing. Then I have to redo parts of it because I realize it doesn’t look right or I forgot what order I was putting things in. It’s not huge, but it’s frustrating. It makes the day feel harder than it needs to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.\n\nAnd the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.\n\nI can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. Like at the front desk, I can still smile and greet people and answer the phone and help them with whatever they need, so I probably look normal enough. Not even a specific thing necessarily, just this feeling like I need to be ready. Then I have to redo parts of it because I realize it doesn’t look right or I forgot what order I was putting things in. It’s not huge, but it’s frustrating. It makes the day feel harder than it needs to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.\n\nAnd the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.\n\nI can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. Like at the front desk, I can still smile and greet people and answer the phone and help them with whatever they need, so I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. I’ll notice my shoulders are all tense, almost like they’re up around my ears, and I’m sitting there waiting for something to go wrong. Not even a specific thing necessarily, just this feeling like I need to be ready. And then if someone walks up suddenly or the phone rings, I’m already kind of on edge before I even know what they need.\n\nSo yes, I guess restless is the right word, but it’s more like tense and keyed up than pacing around or anything dramatic. I’m still doing my job and talking to people, but inside I feel like I can’t fully relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid making decisions or taking actions because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it’s not always that I completely avoid the decision. It’s more that I hesitate, or I put it off, or I make it much bigger in my head than it probably needs to be.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll worry that if I enter something wrong or misunderstand a time, it’s going to create this whole chain of problems for someone else. Or with bills, even ordinary ones, I’ll look at the amount or the due date and think, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are the same way. I can get stuck checking times, addresses, confirmation numbers, because in my mind one little mix-up could turn into a whole mess.\n\nAnd paperwork too, even small things. If there’s a tiny error or I’m not completely sure I wrote something correctly, I can feel myself freezing a bit before I move on, because I’m imagining it turning into a bigger problem later. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can clearly point to. My family and friends know I can be a little… careful, I guess, or that I like to double-check things, but I don’t know if they see it as a serious problem.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I ask for reassurance more than I realize, like, “Did I say that right?” or “Are you sure that’s the time?” But I’m not sure whether that actually bothers them or if I’m just assuming it might. People close to me are usually pretty patient, so it’s hard for me to tell.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a major impact on my relationships. Maybe small things here and there, but nothing obvious enough that someone has said, “This is affecting us,” if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”\n\nAt work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. Like at the front desk, I can still smile and greet people and answer the phone and help them with whatever they need, so I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. Not even a specific thing necessarily, just this feeling like I need to be ready. And then if someone walks up suddenly or the phone rings, I’m already kind of on edge before I even know what they need.\n\nSo yes, I guess restless is the right word, but it’s more like tense and keyed up than pacing around or anything dramatic. I’m still doing my job and talking to people, but inside I feel like I can’t fully relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid making decisions or taking actions because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it’s not always that I completely avoid the decision. It’s more that I hesitate, or I put it off, or I make it much bigger in my head than it probably needs to be.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll worry that if I enter something wrong or misunderstand a time, it’s going to create this whole chain of problems for someone else. Or with bills, even ordinary ones, I’ll look at the amount or the due date and think, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are the same way. I can get stuck checking times, addresses, confirmation numbers, because in my mind one little mix-up could turn into a whole mess.\n\nAnd paperwork too, even small things. If there’s a tiny error or I’m not completely sure I wrote something correctly, I can feel myself freezing a bit before I move on, because I’m imagining it turning into a bigger problem later. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can clearly point to. My family and friends know I can be a little… careful, I guess, or that I like to double-check things, but I don’t know if they see it as a serious problem.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I ask for reassurance more than I realize, like, “Did I say that right?” or “Are you sure that’s the time?” But I’m not sure whether that actually bothers them or if I’m just assuming it might. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. It’s honestly kind of embarrassing because the things are usually so ordinary. It’s not like there’s some obvious crisis happening, but my mind treats it like there could be one if I’m not careful enough.

Like with work schedules, I’ll start worrying that maybe I wrote down the wrong time, or misunderstood what someone told me, and then I picture it causing problems for other people. With bills, even if it’s just a normal bill I pay every month, I’ll find myself checking the due date or the amount and thinking, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are probably another one — I can get caught up in whether the time is right, whether the address is right, whether I should recheck the confirmation.

And it can be really small paperwork things too. Like if I make a tiny error or I’m not completely sure something was filled out correctly, I worry it could turn into some bigger problem later. So yes, I worry about everyday things more than I think makes sense. It’s like one small mix-up feels like it could snowball, even when there isn’t really a clear reason to think that.
