---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.8, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve been having trouble keeping my focus at work. I’m a receptionist, so I’m scheduling appointments and answering questions, and sometimes I’ll be right in the middle of putting something in the calendar or working on the lobby displays, and I just… lose my place. Like my mind slips off to thinking about what I might have missed or overlooked, and then I have to backtrack and check what I was doing.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me. I’ll be trying to concentrate, and then I’m mentally going through, “Did I forget to call someone back? Did I put that in the right spot? Did I miss a detail?” and then I realize I’ve stopped paying attention to the thing right in front of me. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I think I should, honestly. It’s not always one huge thing, either — it’s all these ordinary things that somehow feel like they could go wrong if I don’t keep turning them over in my head.\n\nLike at work, I’ll worry about the schedule, whether I put someone in the right slot or if I missed a note. Or with bills, even if they’re handled, I’ll still think, “What if I forgot one?” If I’m making travel plans, even something simple, I start going through every possible mix-up. And little paperwork errors — things that probably would be fixable — they feel like they could turn into some much bigger problem. I know, logically, that one small mistake usually isn’t the end of the world, but in the moment my mind treats it like it could snowball.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — especially tension. I don’t always notice it right away because I’m still doing my job, you know, smiling at people at the front desk, answering the phone, trying to sound calm and pleasant. But inside I feel really wound tight, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong.\n\nA lot of the time my shoulders are up around my ears without me realizing it. Then at some point I’ll catch myself and try to drop them, but they just creep right back up. It’s like my body is waiting for the next mistake or problem, even if nothing is actually happening in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, like a sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I mean, I can feel very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it builds up and I’ll realize I’m really tense or on edge, but it’s more like it keeps running in the background and ramps up depending on what I’m thinking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had those moments where it’s like, all at once, “Something terrible is happening right now,” and then it peaks within minutes. At least, not that I can clearly point to. Mine feels more drawn out, like I get stuck in the worrying and checking and replaying things, rather than a sudden intense fear spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things exactly… not in a really obvious way, anyway. I still go to work, I still answer the phones, deal with people at the front desk, handle paperwork — I don’t just refuse to do things because I’m worried.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I’ll put things off a little if I’m worried I’m going to mess them up, like I’ll want to check details first or wait until I feel like I have enough time to do it carefully. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.\n\nI don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific acts, like checking or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like a very specific act I have to do a certain number of times or anything like counting or touching things or following a rule.\n\nIt’s more ordinary checking, I guess — like if I’m unsure whether I wrote something down correctly, I might look again, or if I’m worried I misunderstood something, I might review it. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about one specific type of thing or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely spread out. It’s not just one thing, like only money or only work. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”\n\nAt work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. Then at home it might be bills, or making sure a travel plan is right, or whether I forgot some small detail that’s going to snowball into something bigger.\n\nIt’s usually not dramatic things, either. It’s everyday things. But in my head it’s like one small mix-up could turn into a whole chain of problems, and then I start trying to think through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall quality of life or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or not able to do my job, because I do show up, I get through the day, I answer people, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it does interfere, just in these smaller ways that add up. Like scheduling takes me longer than it should now because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at an appointment or a calendar entry and think, “Okay, that’s right,” and then a minute later I’m back checking the same thing again because I don’t trust that I didn’t miss something. So what should be quick ends up taking extra time, and then I feel behind or embarrassed, even if nobody else notices right away.\n\nAnd there have been times with the lobby displays where I’m arranging things, and because I’m so in my head or going back over details, I lose track of what I was doing. Then I have to redo parts of it because I realize it doesn’t look right or I forgot what order I was putting things in. It’s not huge, but it’s frustrating. It makes the day feel harder than it needs to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.\n\nAnd the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.\n\nI can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. Like at the front desk, I can still smile and greet people and answer the phone and help them with whatever they need, so I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. I’ll notice my shoulders are all tense, almost like they’re up around my ears, and I’m sitting there waiting for something to go wrong. Not even a specific thing necessarily, just this feeling like I need to be ready. And then if someone walks up suddenly or the phone rings, I’m already kind of on edge before I even know what they need.\n\nSo yes, I guess restless is the right word, but it’s more like tense and keyed up than pacing around or anything dramatic. I’m still doing my job and talking to people, but inside I feel like I can’t fully relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid making decisions or taking actions because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it’s not always that I completely avoid the decision. It’s more that I hesitate, or I put it off, or I make it much bigger in my head than it probably needs to be.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll worry that if I enter something wrong or misunderstand a time, it’s going to create this whole chain of problems for someone else. Or with bills, even ordinary ones, I’ll look at the amount or the due date and think, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are the same way. I can get stuck checking times, addresses, confirmation numbers, because in my mind one little mix-up could turn into a whole mess.\n\nAnd paperwork too, even small things. If there’s a tiny error or I’m not completely sure I wrote something correctly, I can feel myself freezing a bit before I move on, because I’m imagining it turning into a bigger problem later. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can clearly point to. My family and friends know I can be a little… careful, I guess, or that I like to double-check things, but I don’t know if they see it as a serious problem.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I ask for reassurance more than I realize, like, “Did I say that right?” or “Are you sure that’s the time?” But I’m not sure whether that actually bothers them or if I’m just assuming it might. People close to me are usually pretty patient, so it’s hard for me to tell.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a major impact on my relationships. Maybe small things here and there, but nothing obvious enough that someone has said, “This is affecting us,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I have worries that pop into my head when I don’t want them to, like thinking over something I said or wondering if I forgot something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as an intrusive thought.\n\nWhen you say intrusive or unwanted, I think of something that feels really strange or disturbing and I’m trying hard to push it away, and I don’t really think I have that. It’s more like regular worries that keep coming back, and I’ll try to distract myself or tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this,” but it’s not like I’m scared of the thought itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not in the way I think you mean. I haven’t really noticed specific thoughts that feel intrusive like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like chores or minor decisions, even when there's no obvious reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s honestly kind of embarrassing because the things are usually so ordinary. It’s not like there’s some obvious crisis happening, but my mind treats it like there could be one if I’m not careful enough.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll start worrying that maybe I wrote down the wrong time, or misunderstood what someone told me, and then I picture it causing problems for other people. With bills, even if it’s just a normal bill I pay every month, I’ll find myself checking the due date or the amount and thinking, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are probably another one — I can get caught up in whether the time is right, whether the address is right, whether I should recheck the confirmation.\n\nAnd it can be really small paperwork things too. Like if I make a tiny error or I’m not completely sure something was filled out correctly, I worry it could turn into some bigger problem later. So yes, I worry about everyday things more than I think makes sense. It’s like one small mix-up feels like it could snowball, even when there isn’t really a clear reason to think that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F41.1", "F41.8", "F41.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Yes… I think that does happen, though it feels more like worry getting stuck than one specific thought. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” or “You already checked that,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, I can move on. But then it comes right back around.

Like scheduling takes me longer than it should now because I keep double-checking myself. So what should be quick ends up taking extra time, and then I feel behind or embarrassed, even if nobody else notices right away.\n\nAnd there have been times with the lobby displays where I’m arranging things, and because I’m so in my head or going back over details, I lose track of what I was doing. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. I can get stuck checking times, addresses, confirmation numbers, because in my mind one little mix-up could turn into a whole mess.\n\nAnd paperwork too, even small things. If there’s a tiny error or I’m not completely sure I wrote something correctly, I can feel myself freezing a bit before I move on, because I’m imagining it turning into a bigger problem later. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can clearly point to. My family and friends know I can be a little… careful, I guess, or that I like to double-check things, but I don’t know if they see it as a serious problem.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I ask for reassurance more than I realize, like, “Did I say that right?” or “Are you sure that’s the time?” But I’m not sure whether that actually bothers them or if I’m just assuming it might. People close to me are usually pretty patient, so it’s hard for me to tell.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a major impact on my relationships. Maybe small things here and there, but nothing obvious enough that someone has said, “This is affecting us,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I have worries that pop into my head when I don’t want them to, like thinking over something I said or wondering if I forgot something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as an intrusive thought.\n\nWhen you say intrusive or unwanted, I think of something that feels really strange or disturbing and I’m trying hard to push it away, and I don’t really think I have that. It’s more like regular worries that keep coming back, and I’ll try to distract myself or tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this,” but it’s not like I’m scared of the thought itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not in the way I think you mean. I haven’t really noticed specific thoughts that feel intrusive like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like chores or minor decisions, even when there's no obvious reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s honestly kind of embarrassing because the things are usually so ordinary. It’s not like there’s some obvious crisis happening, but my mind treats it like there could be one if I’m not careful enough.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll start worrying that maybe I wrote down the wrong time, or misunderstood what someone told me, and then I picture it causing problems for other people. With bills, even if it’s just a normal bill I pay every month, I’ll find myself checking the due date or the amount and thinking, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are probably another one — I can get caught up in whether the time is right, whether the address is right, whether I should recheck the confirmation.\n\nAnd it can be really small paperwork things too. Like if I make a tiny error or I’m not completely sure something was filled out correctly, I worry it could turn into some bigger problem later. So yes, I worry about everyday things more than I think makes sense. It’s like one small mix-up feels like it could snowball, even when there isn’t really a clear reason to think that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel like they're stuck in your head and you can't stop thinking about them, even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that does happen, though it feels more like worry getting stuck than one specific thought. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” or “You already checked that,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, I can move on. But then it comes right back around.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing, like whether I got a detail right, and then pretty soon it connects to something else, and then something else. I’ll be going over it in my head, almost like mentally checking a list over and over, even when I don’t really want to be doing that anymore.\n\nIt’s not constant every minute, but when it happens, it’s hard to just drop it. I know logically that I’ve probably already taken care of it, but my mind keeps looping back anyway, like it wants to be completely sure before it lets me relax.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s not like there’s some obvious crisis happening, but my mind treats it like there could be one if I’m not careful enough.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll start worrying that maybe I wrote down the wrong time, or misunderstood what someone told me, and then I picture it causing problems for other people. With bills, even if it’s just a normal bill I pay every month, I’ll find myself checking the due date or the amount and thinking, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are probably another one — I can get caught up in whether the time is right, whether the address is right, whether I should recheck the confirmation.\n\nAnd it can be really small paperwork things too. Like if I make a tiny error or I’m not completely sure something was filled out correctly, I worry it could turn into some bigger problem later. So yes, I worry about everyday things more than I think makes sense. It’s like one small mix-up feels like it could snowball, even when there isn’t really a clear reason to think that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel like they're stuck in your head and you can't stop thinking about them, even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that does happen, though it feels more like worry getting stuck than one specific thought. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” or “You already checked that,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, I can move on. But then it comes right back around.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing, like whether I got a detail right, and then pretty soon it connects to something else, and then something else. I’ll be going over it in my head, almost like mentally checking a list over and over, even when I don’t really want to be doing that anymore.\n\nIt’s not constant every minute, but when it happens, it’s hard to just drop it. I know logically that I’ve probably already taken care of it, but my mind keeps looping back anyway, like it wants to be completely sure before it lets me relax.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your worrying is affecting your ability to enjoy your free time or pursue hobbies?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 44 / receptionist_or_information_clerk / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still showing up and getting through the day, but scheduling takes me longer now because I double-check myself so much, and I’ve had to redo parts of the lobby displays after losing track of what I was arranging.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… somewhat. I wouldn’t say it’s completely stopping me from doing things, because I’m still showing up, still getting through the day, still doing what I need to do. But it does sort of eat into my time and my attention more than I want to admit.

Like at work, scheduling takes me longer than it used to because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at the time, then look again, then compare it to what I wrote somewhere else, and it’s not that I don’t know how to do it — I do — but I get worried I’ve missed something small. So something that should be quick ends up taking extra time.

And even with things that are supposed to be more enjoyable or creative, like arranging the lobby displays, I’ve had times where I lose track of what I was doing because I’m second-guessing a detail or thinking back over something else. Then I realize I’ve kind of messed up the arrangement or forgotten where I was, and I have to redo parts of it. It’s frustrating, because that kind of thing should feel satisfying, and instead I’m spending more time fixing it or checking it than enjoying it.

So… yes, it affects my free time and hobbies a little, mostly because I have less mental space left over. It’s not like I’m unable to function, but things take longer, and I don’t feel as relaxed as I think I should.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve been having trouble keeping my focus at work. I’ll be trying to concentrate, and then I’m mentally going through, “Did I forget to call someone back? Did I put that in the right spot? Did I miss a detail?” and then I realize I’ve stopped paying attention to the thing right in front of me. It’s not always one huge thing, either — it’s all these ordinary things that somehow feel like they could go wrong if I don’t keep turning them over in my head.\n\nLike at work, I’ll worry about the schedule, whether I put someone in the right slot or if I missed a note. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.\n\nI don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific acts, like checking or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like a very specific act I have to do a certain number of times or anything like counting or touching things or following a rule.\n\nIt’s more ordinary checking, I guess — like if I’m unsure whether I wrote something down correctly, I might look again, or if I’m worried I misunderstood something, I might review it. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about one specific type of thing or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely spread out. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”\n\nAt work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. Then at home it might be bills, or making sure a travel plan is right, or whether I forgot some small detail that’s going to snowball into something bigger.\n\nIt’s usually not dramatic things, either. It’s everyday things. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or not able to do my job, because I do show up, I get through the day, I answer people, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it does interfere, just in these smaller ways that add up. Like scheduling takes me longer than it should now because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at an appointment or a calendar entry and think, “Okay, that’s right,” and then a minute later I’m back checking the same thing again because I don’t trust that I didn’t miss something. So what should be quick ends up taking extra time, and then I feel behind or embarrassed, even if nobody else notices right away.\n\nAnd there have been times with the lobby displays where I’m arranging things, and because I’m so in my head or going back over details, I lose track of what I was doing. It makes the day feel harder than it needs to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.\n\nAnd the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.\n\nI can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. I can get stuck checking times, addresses, confirmation numbers, because in my mind one little mix-up could turn into a whole mess.\n\nAnd paperwork too, even small things. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. My family and friends know I can be a little… careful, I guess, or that I like to double-check things, but I don’t know if they see it as a serious problem.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I ask for reassurance more than I realize, like, “Did I say that right?” or “Are you sure that’s the time?” But I’m not sure whether that actually bothers them or if I’m just assuming it might. People close to me are usually pretty patient, so it’s hard for me to tell.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a major impact on my relationships. Maybe small things here and there, but nothing obvious enough that someone has said, “This is affecting us,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I have worries that pop into my head when I don’t want them to, like thinking over something I said or wondering if I forgot something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as an intrusive thought.\n\nWhen you say intrusive or unwanted, I think of something that feels really strange or disturbing and I’m trying hard to push it away, and I don’t really think I have that. It’s more like regular worries that keep coming back, and I’ll try to distract myself or tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this,” but it’s not like I’m scared of the thought itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not in the way I think you mean. I haven’t really noticed specific thoughts that feel intrusive like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like chores or minor decisions, even when there's no obvious reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like there’s some obvious crisis happening, but my mind treats it like there could be one if I’m not careful enough.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll start worrying that maybe I wrote down the wrong time, or misunderstood what someone told me, and then I picture it causing problems for other people. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”\n\nAt work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. Then at home it might be bills, or making sure a travel plan is right, or whether I forgot some small detail that’s going to snowball into something bigger.\n\nIt’s usually not dramatic things, either. It’s everyday things. But in my head it’s like one small mix-up could turn into a whole chain of problems, and then I start trying to think through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall quality of life or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or not able to do my job, because I do show up, I get through the day, I answer people, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it does interfere, just in these smaller ways that add up. Like scheduling takes me longer than it should now because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at an appointment or a calendar entry and think, “Okay, that’s right,” and then a minute later I’m back checking the same thing again because I don’t trust that I didn’t miss something. So what should be quick ends up taking extra time, and then I feel behind or embarrassed, even if nobody else notices right away.\n\nAnd there have been times with the lobby displays where I’m arranging things, and because I’m so in my head or going back over details, I lose track of what I was doing. Then I have to redo parts of it because I realize it doesn’t look right or I forgot what order I was putting things in. It’s not huge, but it’s frustrating. It makes the day feel harder than it needs to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.\n\nAnd the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.\n\nI can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. Like at the front desk, I can still smile and greet people and answer the phone and help them with whatever they need, so I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. I’ll notice my shoulders are all tense, almost like they’re up around my ears, and I’m sitting there waiting for something to go wrong. Not even a specific thing necessarily, just this feeling like I need to be ready. And then if someone walks up suddenly or the phone rings, I’m already kind of on edge before I even know what they need.\n\nSo yes, I guess restless is the right word, but it’s more like tense and keyed up than pacing around or anything dramatic. I’m still doing my job and talking to people, but inside I feel like I can’t fully relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid making decisions or taking actions because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it’s not always that I completely avoid the decision. It’s more that I hesitate, or I put it off, or I make it much bigger in my head than it probably needs to be.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll worry that if I enter something wrong or misunderstand a time, it’s going to create this whole chain of problems for someone else. Or with bills, even ordinary ones, I’ll look at the amount or the due date and think, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are the same way. I can get stuck checking times, addresses, confirmation numbers, because in my mind one little mix-up could turn into a whole mess.\n\nAnd paperwork too, even small things. If there’s a tiny error or I’m not completely sure I wrote something correctly, I can feel myself freezing a bit before I move on, because I’m imagining it turning into a bigger problem later. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can clearly point to. My family and friends know I can be a little… careful, I guess, or that I like to double-check things, but I don’t know if they see it as a serious problem.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I ask for reassurance more than I realize, like, “Did I say that right?” or “Are you sure that’s the time?” But I’m not sure whether that actually bothers them or if I’m just assuming it might. People close to me are usually pretty patient, so it’s hard for me to tell.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a major impact on my relationships. Maybe small things here and there, but nothing obvious enough that someone has said, “This is affecting us,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I have worries that pop into my head when I don’t want them to, like thinking over something I said or wondering if I forgot something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as an intrusive thought.\n\nWhen you say intrusive or unwanted, I think of something that feels really strange or disturbing and I’m trying hard to push it away, and I don’t really think I have that. It’s more like regular worries that keep coming back, and I’ll try to distract myself or tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this,” but it’s not like I’m scared of the thought itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not in the way I think you mean. I haven’t really noticed specific thoughts that feel intrusive like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like chores or minor decisions, even when there's no obvious reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s honestly kind of embarrassing because the things are usually so ordinary. It’s not like there’s some obvious crisis happening, but my mind treats it like there could be one if I’m not careful enough.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll start worrying that maybe I wrote down the wrong time, or misunderstood what someone told me, and then I picture it causing problems for other people. With bills, even if it’s just a normal bill I pay every month, I’ll find myself checking the due date or the amount and thinking, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are probably another one — I can get caught up in whether the time is right, whether the address is right, whether I should recheck the confirmation.\n\nAnd it can be really small paperwork things too. Like if I make a tiny error or I’m not completely sure something was filled out correctly, I worry it could turn into some bigger problem later. So yes, I worry about everyday things more than I think makes sense. It’s like one small mix-up feels like it could snowball, even when there isn’t really a clear reason to think that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel like they're stuck in your head and you can't stop thinking about them, even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that does happen, though it feels more like worry getting stuck than one specific thought. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” or “You already checked that,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, I can move on. But then it comes right back around.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing, like whether I got a detail right, and then pretty soon it connects to something else, and then something else. I’ll be going over it in my head, almost like mentally checking a list over and over, even when I don’t really want to be doing that anymore.\n\nIt’s not constant every minute, but when it happens, it’s hard to just drop it. I know logically that I’ve probably already taken care of it, but my mind keeps looping back anyway, like it wants to be completely sure before it lets me relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is affecting your ability to enjoy your free time or pursue hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I wouldn’t say it’s completely stopping me from doing things, because I’m still showing up, still getting through the day, still doing what I need to do. But it does sort of eat into my time and my attention more than I want to admit.\n\nLike at work, scheduling takes me longer than it used to because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at the time, then look again, then compare it to what I wrote somewhere else, and it’s not that I don’t know how to do it — I do — but I get worried I’ve missed something small. So something that should be quick ends up taking extra time.\n\nAnd even with things that are supposed to be more enjoyable or creative, like arranging the lobby displays, I’ve had times where I lose track of what I was doing because I’m second-guessing a detail or thinking back over something else. Then I realize I’ve kind of messed up the arrangement or forgotten where I was, and I have to redo parts of it. It’s frustrating, because that kind of thing should feel satisfying, and instead I’m spending more time fixing it or checking it than enjoying it.\n\nSo… yes, it affects my free time and hobbies a little, mostly because I have less mental space left over. It’s more than just one area, I think. It kind of spreads around to a lot of ordinary life stuff. Work is definitely one, especially schedules or whether I understood a detail correctly. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.8, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I’ve been having trouble keeping my focus at work. Like my mind slips off to thinking about what I might have missed or overlooked, and then I have to backtrack and check what I was doing.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t do my job, exactly, but it’s been happening enough that it’s bothering me. I’ll be trying to concentrate, and then I’m mentally going through, “Did I forget to call someone back? Did I put that in the right spot? Did I miss a detail?” and then I realize I’ve stopped paying attention to the thing right in front of me. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you think you should?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I think I should, honestly. It’s not always one huge thing, either — it’s all these ordinary things that somehow feel like they could go wrong if I don’t keep turning them over in my head.\n\nLike at work, I’ll worry about the schedule, whether I put someone in the right slot or if I missed a note. Or with bills, even if they’re handled, I’ll still think, “What if I forgot one?” If I’m making travel plans, even something simple, I start going through every possible mix-up. And little paperwork errors — things that probably would be fixable — they feel like they could turn into some much bigger problem. I know, logically, that one small mistake usually isn’t the end of the world, but in the moment my mind treats it like it could snowball.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or muscle tension when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — especially tension. I don’t always notice it right away because I’m still doing my job, you know, smiling at people at the front desk, answering the phone, trying to sound calm and pleasant. But inside I feel really wound tight, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong.\n\nA lot of the time my shoulders are up around my ears without me realizing it. Then at some point I’ll catch myself and try to drop them, but they just creep right back up. It’s like my body is waiting for the next mistake or problem, even if nothing is actually happening in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, like a sudden wave that comes out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I mean, I can feel very uncomfortable when I’m worrying, and sometimes it builds up and I’ll realize I’m really tense or on edge, but it’s more like it keeps running in the background and ramps up depending on what I’m thinking about.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had those moments where it’s like, all at once, “Something terrible is happening right now,” and then it peaks within minutes. At least, not that I can clearly point to. Mine feels more drawn out, like I get stuck in the worrying and checking and replaying things, rather than a sudden intense fear spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or situations because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I avoid things exactly… not in a really obvious way, anyway. I still go to work, I still answer the phones, deal with people at the front desk, handle paperwork — I don’t just refuse to do things because I’m worried.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I’ll put things off a little if I’m worried I’m going to mess them up, like I’ll want to check details first or wait until I feel like I have enough time to do it carefully. It’s more that I slow myself down than completely avoid it. I might keep rereading something or double-checking before I send it or turn it in, because I don’t want to create a problem for someone else.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m avoiding whole situations, no. It’s more like I get caught up in making sure I’ve done things right, and that can make me hesitate.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions or thoughts over and over, even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… but it’s more in my head than like doing some obvious ritual, if that makes sense. I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You already checked it,” or, “You already answered that question,” and for a second I believe it. But then the thought comes right back around.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing — did I give the right information, did I word that message clearly, did I forget some little detail — and then before I know it, I’ve moved on to three other concerns. It kind of loops. I’ll be checking details in my head over and over, almost like I’m trying to prove to myself that everything is okay, but it doesn’t really settle me for long.\n\nI don’t always want to be doing it. Actually, most of the time I’m tired of it. I’ll think, “Just stop, it’s fine,” but my mind keeps going back through it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific acts, like checking or counting, in response to your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel compelled in that way, no. I mean, I do check things sometimes, but it doesn’t feel like a very specific act I have to do a certain number of times or anything like counting or touching things or following a rule.\n\nIt’s more ordinary checking, I guess — like if I’m unsure whether I wrote something down correctly, I might look again, or if I’m worried I misunderstood something, I might review it. But I wouldn’t say I have a set routine where I think, “I have to do this or something bad will happen.” It’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself that I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… no counting. No specific ritual that I can think of. Just a lot of going back over things when I’m uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries usually about one specific type of thing or are they spread across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They’re definitely spread out. It’s not just one thing, like only money or only work. It can be all kinds of ordinary stuff, and that’s part of what makes it frustrating, because sometimes I’ll think, “Why am I turning this into such a big deal?”\n\nAt work it might be the schedule, or whether I gave someone the right information, or if a little paperwork mistake is going to cause trouble later. Then at home it might be bills, or making sure a travel plan is right, or whether I forgot some small detail that’s going to snowball into something bigger.\n\nIt’s usually not dramatic things, either. It’s everyday things. But in my head it’s like one small mix-up could turn into a whole chain of problems, and then I start trying to think through every possible way it could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is interfering with your overall quality of life or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or not able to do my job, because I do show up, I get through the day, I answer people, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it does interfere, just in these smaller ways that add up. Like scheduling takes me longer than it should now because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at an appointment or a calendar entry and think, “Okay, that’s right,” and then a minute later I’m back checking the same thing again because I don’t trust that I didn’t miss something. So what should be quick ends up taking extra time, and then I feel behind or embarrassed, even if nobody else notices right away.\n\nAnd there have been times with the lobby displays where I’m arranging things, and because I’m so in my head or going back over details, I lose track of what I was doing. Then I have to redo parts of it because I realize it doesn’t look right or I forgot what order I was putting things in. It’s not huge, but it’s frustrating. It makes the day feel harder than it needs to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. It’s not usually like one strange thought out of nowhere, it’s more like a concern gets stuck and keeps circling back even after I’ve already dealt with it.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled. You checked it. You wrote it down. It’s fine.” And for maybe a moment I’ll believe that, but then the thought comes right back, like, “But what if you wrote the wrong time?” or “What if you misunderstood what they said?” Then I start replaying the details in my head again, almost like I’m reviewing a little mental checklist, even when I’m trying to move on.\n\nAnd the annoying part is it doesn’t always stay on the first thing. It’ll jump. So I’ll start with one worry, like an appointment time, and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether I gave someone the right information, and then whether I forgot something else, and before I know it I’ve gone from one concern to another and I’m checking the same details in my head over and over.\n\nI can usually keep doing what I need to do, but it feels like there’s this extra track running in the background that I can’t quite shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think. Like at the front desk, I can still smile and greet people and answer the phone and help them with whatever they need, so I probably look normal enough. But inside I feel really wound tight a lot of the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for something, even if nothing is actually happening right then. I’ll notice my shoulders are all tense, almost like they’re up around my ears, and I’m sitting there waiting for something to go wrong. Not even a specific thing necessarily, just this feeling like I need to be ready. And then if someone walks up suddenly or the phone rings, I’m already kind of on edge before I even know what they need.\n\nSo yes, I guess restless is the right word, but it’s more like tense and keyed up than pacing around or anything dramatic. I’m still doing my job and talking to people, but inside I feel like I can’t fully relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid making decisions or taking actions because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though it’s not always that I completely avoid the decision. It’s more that I hesitate, or I put it off, or I make it much bigger in my head than it probably needs to be.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll worry that if I enter something wrong or misunderstand a time, it’s going to create this whole chain of problems for someone else. Or with bills, even ordinary ones, I’ll look at the amount or the due date and think, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are the same way. I can get stuck checking times, addresses, confirmation numbers, because in my mind one little mix-up could turn into a whole mess.\n\nAnd paperwork too, even small things. If there’s a tiny error or I’m not completely sure I wrote something correctly, I can feel myself freezing a bit before I move on, because I’m imagining it turning into a bigger problem later. So I still do things, usually, but it takes me longer because I’m trying so hard not to make a mistake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your worrying affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can clearly point to. My family and friends know I can be a little… careful, I guess, or that I like to double-check things, but I don’t know if they see it as a serious problem.\n\nSometimes I wonder if I ask for reassurance more than I realize, like, “Did I say that right?” or “Are you sure that’s the time?” But I’m not sure whether that actually bothers them or if I’m just assuming it might. People close to me are usually pretty patient, so it’s hard for me to tell.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a major impact on my relationships. Maybe small things here and there, but nothing obvious enough that someone has said, “This is affecting us,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted, and that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I have worries that pop into my head when I don’t want them to, like thinking over something I said or wondering if I forgot something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as an intrusive thought.\n\nWhen you say intrusive or unwanted, I think of something that feels really strange or disturbing and I’m trying hard to push it away, and I don’t really think I have that. It’s more like regular worries that keep coming back, and I’ll try to distract myself or tell myself, “Okay, stop thinking about this,” but it’s not like I’m scared of the thought itself, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not in the way I think you mean. I haven’t really noticed specific thoughts that feel intrusive like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like chores or minor decisions, even when there's no obvious reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s honestly kind of embarrassing because the things are usually so ordinary. It’s not like there’s some obvious crisis happening, but my mind treats it like there could be one if I’m not careful enough.\n\nLike with work schedules, I’ll start worrying that maybe I wrote down the wrong time, or misunderstood what someone told me, and then I picture it causing problems for other people. With bills, even if it’s just a normal bill I pay every month, I’ll find myself checking the due date or the amount and thinking, “What if I missed something?” Travel plans are probably another one — I can get caught up in whether the time is right, whether the address is right, whether I should recheck the confirmation.\n\nAnd it can be really small paperwork things too. Like if I make a tiny error or I’m not completely sure something was filled out correctly, I worry it could turn into some bigger problem later. So yes, I worry about everyday things more than I think makes sense. It’s like one small mix-up feels like it could snowball, even when there isn’t really a clear reason to think that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that feel like they're stuck in your head and you can't stop thinking about them, even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that does happen, though it feels more like worry getting stuck than one specific thought. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” or “You already checked that,” and for a second I’ll feel like, okay, I can move on. But then it comes right back around.\n\nIt’ll start with one thing, like whether I got a detail right, and then pretty soon it connects to something else, and then something else. I’ll be going over it in my head, almost like mentally checking a list over and over, even when I don’t really want to be doing that anymore.\n\nIt’s not constant every minute, but when it happens, it’s hard to just drop it. I know logically that I’ve probably already taken care of it, but my mind keeps looping back anyway, like it wants to be completely sure before it lets me relax.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is affecting your ability to enjoy your free time or pursue hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I wouldn’t say it’s completely stopping me from doing things, because I’m still showing up, still getting through the day, still doing what I need to do. But it does sort of eat into my time and my attention more than I want to admit.\n\nLike at work, scheduling takes me longer than it used to because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll look at the time, then look again, then compare it to what I wrote somewhere else, and it’s not that I don’t know how to do it — I do — but I get worried I’ve missed something small. So something that should be quick ends up taking extra time.\n\nAnd even with things that are supposed to be more enjoyable or creative, like arranging the lobby displays, I’ve had times where I lose track of what I was doing because I’m second-guessing a detail or thinking back over something else. Then I realize I’ve kind of messed up the arrangement or forgotten where I was, and I have to redo parts of it. It’s frustrating, because that kind of thing should feel satisfying, and instead I’m spending more time fixing it or checking it than enjoying it.\n\nSo… yes, it affects my free time and hobbies a little, mostly because I have less mental space left over. It’s not like I’m unable to function, but things take longer, and I don’t feel as relaxed as I think I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about most areas of your life, such as work, finances, relationships, and health, or is it mainly focused on one or two areas?\n\nPatient: It’s more than just one area, I think. It kind of spreads around to a lot of ordinary life stuff. Work is definitely one, especially schedules or whether I understood a detail correctly. But then it’s bills too, like whether I paid the right amount or got the due date right, even when it’s something routine.\n\nAnd travel plans — even simple ones — I’ll worry about the time, the address, the confirmation, all of that. Paperwork is another big one, even small errors. I’ll think, “What if this tiny mistake turns into something bigger later?” So it’s not just, say, finances or just work. It’s more like my mind treats lots of normal things as if one small mix-up could snowball into a bigger problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I’d say it’s interfering, but maybe not in a dramatic way. I’m still showing up. I’m still going to work, answering phones, helping people, doing the things I’m supposed to do. It’s not like I’m missing work or completely unable to function.

But it does slow me down more than it used to. Scheduling especially takes me longer now because I keep double-checking myself. I’ll enter something or write it down, and then I’ll go back and look again, sometimes more than once, because I get this feeling like maybe I copied the time wrong or missed some small detail. So a task that should be straightforward ends up taking up extra time and energy.

And with the lobby displays, which I usually like doing, I’ve had times where I lose track of what I was arranging because my mind is caught on some detail or I’m second-guessing something. Then I realize it doesn’t look right or I forgot where I was in the setup, and I have to redo parts of it. That feels frustrating, because I’m getting through the day, but it’s like everything takes more effort than it should. So… significant? Maybe mildly significant. Not ruining my life, but definitely making daily things harder.

Did I miss a detail?” and then I realize I’ve stopped paying attention to the thing right in front of me.
